# <span id="id1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">VEX-Bench</span>: Benchmarking LLM Agents for Assessing Exploitability of Software Supply Chain Vulnerabilities

<div id="abstract1" class="ltx_abstract">

###### Abstract

The software supply chain has become an increasingly exposed attack surface because of its reliance on intricate yet fragile dependencies. Existing defenses such as GitHub Dependabot often raise many false alerts because their coarse-grained matching cannot determine whether a vulnerable dependency is actually exploitable. Security analysts typically spend substantial time assessing vulnerability exploitability case by case. Recent LLM agents have emerged as promising candidates for this task given their advanced capabilities in coding and cybersecurity, yet no existing benchmark evaluates them on it. Prior benchmarks target zero-day settings, where agents detect and exploit previously unknown vulnerabilities. In contrast, software supply chain security focuses on how known vulnerabilities in upstream dependencies affect downstream projects. This requires agents to reason across repositories and determine whether an upstream vulnerability is exploitable in the downstream project. To address this gap, we introduce <span id="abstract1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">VEX-Bench</span>, the first benchmark for evaluating LLM agents’ ability to assess the exploitability of software supply chain vulnerabilities. It contains 75 real-world cases mined from GitHub and labeled by security experts, covering Python, Java, and Go. We evaluate nine models across three agent harnesses. While GPT-5.5 and Claude Opus 4.6 reach approximately 80% F1 on binary vulnerability-status classification, only GPT-5.5 surpasses 70% macro-F1 on fine-grained justification classification. This gap highlights the challenge of moving beyond binary exploitability assessment to identifying fine-grained exploitability reasons. Code and data: <a href="https://github.com/steven1518/vex-bench" class="ltx_ref ltx_url ltx_font_typewriter">https://github.com/steven1518/vex-bench</a>

</div>

<div id="p1" class="ltx_para ltx_align_bottom">

<span id="p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_inline-block" style="width:0.0pt;"></span>

</div>

<div id="S1" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">1 </span>Introduction

<div id="S1.p1" class="ltx_para">

The software supply chain faces increasingly severe security threats. Sonatype reports that software supply chain attacks have grown 156% year-over-year (<a href="#bib.bib8" class="ltx_ref">Sonatype, 2024</a>), and the 2025 OWASP Top 10 ranks *Software Supply Chain Failures* third overall, with 50% of surveyed practitioners ranking it their top concern (<a href="#bib.bib1" class="ltx_ref">OWASP Foundation, 2025</a>). A single vulnerability, Log4Shell (<a href="#bib.bib2" class="ltx_ref">NVD, 2021</a>), affected hundreds of millions of devices and 93% of enterprise cloud environments, causing billions of dollars in industry-wide remediation costs. This growing severity stems largely from modern software’s reliance on expansive package ecosystems (e.g., npm, PyPI, Maven) to reduce development costs and accelerate delivery. This creates complex dependencies among packages, allowing a single vulnerable package to propagate risk into a vast number of downstream projects.

</div>

<div id="S1.p2" class="ltx_para">

To defend against such risks, existing tools such as GitHub Dependabot (<a href="#bib.bib15" class="ltx_ref">Dependabot, 2026</a>) scan software components against vulnerability databases to identify known vulnerabilities. However, such coarse-grained matching produces a large number of false alerts, as it relies primarily on package names and versions, without considering contextual information or fine-grained reachability analysis (<a href="#bib.bib10" class="ltx_ref">Pu et al., 2026</a>; <a href="#bib.bib14" class="ltx_ref">Pashchenko et al., 2022</a>). Security analysts are often overwhelmed by these alerts and spend substantial effort triaging them, leading to alert fatigue and causing critical vulnerabilities to be buried among spurious ones (<a href="#bib.bib12" class="ltx_ref">He et al., 2023</a>; <a href="#bib.bib13" class="ltx_ref">Rombaut et al., 2023</a>; <a href="#bib.bib11" class="ltx_ref">Zhou et al., 2026</a>). These limitations motivate leveraging LLM agents, whose strong coding and cybersecurity capabilities (<a href="#bib.bib16" class="ltx_ref">Anthropic, 2026a</a>; <a href="#bib.bib17" class="ltx_ref">OpenAI, 2026b</a>) make them well-suited for assessing exploitability of software supply chain vulnerabilities.

</div>

<div id="S1.p3" class="ltx_para">

However, existing cybersecurity benchmarks for evaluating LLM agents (<a href="#bib.bib6" class="ltx_ref">Zhang et al., 2026</a>; <a href="#bib.bib3" class="ltx_ref">Wang et al., 2026</a>; <a href="#bib.bib4" class="ltx_ref">Lee et al., 2026</a>; <a href="#bib.bib5" class="ltx_ref">Zhu et al., 2025</a>; <a href="#bib.bib7" class="ltx_ref">Zhang et al., 2025</a>) focus on zero-day settings, where agents discover or exploit previously unknown vulnerabilities within a single codebase. In contrast, software supply chain security poses a completely different setting—the vulnerability is already known and is introduced into the local project through a third-party dependency. This task therefore requires agents to integrate external vulnerability evidence with cross-repository reasoning to assess downstream exposure.

</div>

<div id="S1.p4" class="ltx_para">

To address this gap, we construct <span id="S1.p4.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_smallcaps">VEX-Bench</span>, the first benchmark for evaluating LLM agents’ ability to assess the exploitability of software supply chain vulnerabilities. <span id="S1.p4.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">VEX-Bench</span> contains 75 real-world cases mined from GitHub across Go, Python, and Java projects, with ground-truth labels manually annotated by security experts. Given a codebase and a known vulnerability in one of its dependencies, an agent must determine whether the codebase is genuinely affected. Beyond the binary affected/not-affected decision, we define fine-grained justification labels that explain why a downstream project is not affected, mirroring how vulnerability status is communicated in industry. These choices together enable a more realistic evaluation of LLM agents on real-world software supply chain tasks.

</div>

<div id="S1.p5" class="ltx_para">

We evaluate nine models across three agent harnesses on <span id="S1.p5.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">VEX-Bench</span>. For binary vulnerability-status classification, Claude Opus 4.6 and GPT-5.5 achieve the highest status F1 scores, reaching 81.6% and 79.9%, respectively. Performance drops at the finer justification granularity: only GPT-5.5 exceeds 70% macro-F1. This indicates that identifying the precise exploitability reason is more challenging than making the coarse affected/not-affected decision. We further analyze performance across inference cost, programming language, repository size, and harness choice, providing a more detailed picture of where fine-grained exploitability reasoning remains difficult.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S2" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">2 </span>Related Work

<div id="S2.SS1" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">2.1 </span>Software Supply Chain Security

<div id="S2.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

Many known vulnerabilities propagate risk to downstream projects through software supply chains (<a href="#bib.bib32" class="ltx_ref">Wu et al., 2023</a>; <a href="#bib.bib33" class="ltx_ref">Shen et al., 2025</a>). Software composition analysis (SCA) tools automatically identify third-party components in a project and flag vulnerable dependencies by matching package names and versions against vulnerability databases (<a href="#bib.bib28" class="ltx_ref">Ponta et al., 2020</a>; <a href="#bib.bib29" class="ltx_ref">Imtiaz et al., 2021</a>; <a href="#bib.bib34" class="ltx_ref">Zhao et al., 2023</a>). However, such metadata-based matching is often imprecise and generates many false alerts (<a href="#bib.bib14" class="ltx_ref">Pashchenko et al., 2022</a>; <a href="#bib.bib10" class="ltx_ref">Pu et al., 2026</a>). Another line of work uses reachability analysis to filter alerts whose vulnerable functions are never invoked (<a href="#bib.bib28" class="ltx_ref">Ponta et al., 2020</a>; <a href="#bib.bib31" class="ltx_ref">Jia et al., 2025</a>). However, reachability analysis still faces several limitations. Identifying the exact vulnerable functions is itself challenging (<a href="#bib.bib30" class="ltx_ref">Dunlap et al., 2024</a>). Call graphs are difficult to make both sound and precise. Most importantly, reachability does not imply exploitability, which may additionally depend on configuration and environmental conditions.

</div>

<div id="S2.SS1.p2" class="ltx_para">

In this work, we propose <span id="S2.SS1.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">VEX-Bench</span> to evaluate whether LLM agents can perform end-to-end exploitability assessments of vulnerable dependencies in downstream projects.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S2.SS2" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">2.2 </span>Cybersecurity Benchmarks for Agents

<div id="S2.SS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

Recent benchmarks evaluate LLM agents on cybersecurity tasks from two main angles. CyBench (<a href="#bib.bib7" class="ltx_ref">Zhang et al., 2025</a>) uses Capture the Flag (CTF) challenges, where agents operate in compact, self-contained environments and recover hidden flags by exploiting intentionally planted vulnerabilities. Other benchmarks use real-world software projects, drawing from historical CVEs, OSS-Fuzz reports, or bug-bounty disclosures to ask agents to detect, exploit, or patch vulnerabilities in the repositories that contain the flawed code (<a href="#bib.bib3" class="ltx_ref">Wang et al., 2026</a>; <a href="#bib.bib4" class="ltx_ref">Lee et al., 2026</a>; <a href="#bib.bib5" class="ltx_ref">Zhu et al., 2025</a>; <a href="#bib.bib6" class="ltx_ref">Zhang et al., 2026</a>).

</div>

<div id="S2.SS2.p2" class="ltx_para">

These benchmarks primarily focus on *first-party* vulnerabilities within a single codebase, overlooking software supply chain vulnerabilities. <span id="S2.SS2.p2.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">VEX-Bench</span> instead studies dependency-level exploitability assessment: given a downstream project and a known CVE in one of its third-party dependencies, the agent must determine whether the project is actually affected by searching for upstream vulnerability information and inspecting downstream dependency usage.

</div>

<figure id="S2.F1" class="ltx_figure">
<img src="2609.08040v1/vex-bench.png" id="S2.F1.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_landscape" style="aspect-ratio:429/231;" width="429" height="231" alt="Refer to caption" />
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 1: </span><span id="S2.F1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">VEX-Bench</span> sources task instances from real-world projects by pairing codebases with third-party dependency vulnerabilities (CVEs) to determine real-world exploitability. Provided with the project’s source code and CVE identifier, LLM agents search external information and analyze code to produce a vulnerability status (Affected / Not Affected), a justification label (e.g., code_not_reachable), and reasoning grounded in the codebase.</figcaption>
</figure>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">3 </span><span id="S3.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">VEX-Bench</span>

<div id="S3.p1" class="ltx_para">

We construct <span id="S3.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">VEX-Bench</span> to evaluate whether LLM-based agents can determine if a project is genuinely affected by a known vulnerability in its dependencies. The task requires agents to analyze the local codebase, retrieve information from external sources, inspect upstream projects and vulnerability reports, and synthesize these signals into an end-to-end exploitability analysis. This assessment is routinely performed by security analysts, and its results are communicated through the Vulnerability Exploitability eXchange (VEX) format (<a href="#bib.bib9" class="ltx_ref">NTIA, 2021</a>), a machine-readable specification for reporting whether a known vulnerability actually affects a given product.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS1" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">3.1 </span>Task Formulation

<div id="S3.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

We formalize the task as cross-repository vulnerability exploitability assessment. As shown in <a href="#S2.F1" class="ltx_ref" title="In 2.2 Cybersecurity Benchmarks for Agents ‣ 2 Related Work ‣ VEX-Bench: Benchmarking LLM Agents for Assessing Exploitability of Software Supply Chain Vulnerabilities"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Figure</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>, the agent operates within a target codebase as its working environment and is prompted with a CVE identifier for a known vulnerability in dependencies. The agent must analyze the codebase against the vulnerability and produce a structured prediction together with supporting evidence.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS1.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Input Formulation

<div id="S3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">

Each task consists of a *target codebase* and a *vulnerability identifier*. The codebase is a complete real-world project that serves as the agent’s working environment, in which it reads files, runs commands, and analyzes code. The vulnerability identifier is a CVE referring to a known issue in one of the codebase’s third-party dependencies, and is delivered to the agent as part of its instruction prompt. The full prompt template is provided in Appendix <a href="#A2.SS1" class="ltx_ref" title="B.1 Task Instruction Prompt ‣ Appendix B Prompts ‣ VEX-Bench: Benchmarking LLM Agents for Assessing Exploitability of Software Supply Chain Vulnerabilities"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">B.1</span></a>.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p2" class="ltx_para">

By design, the agent receives no curated advisory text, no preselected vulnerable files, and no pre-computed call graph slice. It must retrieve information about the vulnerability from external sources and locate the relevant code paths in the codebase without assistance. This mirrors the situation a human expert faces when a scanner flags a production repository, and ensures that our evaluation reflects the agent’s own cross-source reasoning rather than the leverage of human-curated hints.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS1.SSS0.Px2" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Output Formulation

<div id="S3.SS1.SSS0.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para">

The agent produces a structured output with three fields. The *vulnerability status* is a binary label indicating whether the codebase is affected. When the status is not-affected, the *justification* further specifies the reason, drawn from four fine-grained not-affected categories. The *reasoning trace* is a free-form explanation recording which call sites the agent inspected, which input paths it followed, and which mitigations it identified — making the prediction auditable rather than a black-box verdict. Together, these fields support both quick triage on the binary status and deeper review through the justification and trace.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS1.SSS0.Px3" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Justification Labels

<div id="S3.SS1.SSS0.Px3.p1" class="ltx_para">

Although the exploitability assessment is binary at the top level, a single <span id="S3.SS1.SSS0.Px3.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">not_affected</span> status can arise from different reasons. In one case the vulnerable function may never be invoked along any reachable call path; in another, the exploit may require preconditions that the project’s default configuration prevents. Distinguishing these cases serves two purposes. First, it has direct practical value, since different reasons imply different follow-up actions for the downstream maintainer, and the specific reason an agent identifies reveals which kind of reasoning it is actually performing. Second, it is necessary for reliable evaluation. <span id="S3.SS1.SSS0.Px3.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">not_affected</span> cases dominate real-world triage outcomes, so the dataset is heavily imbalanced and a binary status prediction is easy to inflate by simply predicting the majority class. Our schema is adapted from the VEX standard (<a href="#bib.bib27" class="ltx_ref">CISA, 2022</a>). <span id="S3.SS1.SSS0.Px3.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">VEX-Bench</span> defines four justification categories that capture the most common reasons a known vulnerability fails to be exploitable in a downstream project. Detailed definitions and examples for each category appear in Appendix <a href="#A1" class="ltx_ref" title="Appendix A Justification Category Definitions ‣ VEX-Bench: Benchmarking LLM Agents for Assessing Exploitability of Software Supply Chain Vulnerabilities"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">A</span></a>.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">3.2 </span>Benchmark Construction

<div id="S3.SS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

We construct <span id="S3.SS2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">VEX-Bench</span> through three stages: (1) mining candidate cases from GitHub, (2) rule-based filtering, and (3) manual annotation.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Mining GitHub Data

<div id="S3.SS2.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">

We begin by targeting popular and mature open-source projects on GitHub, as such projects better reflect real-world software complexity, active maintenance, and practical dependency management scenarios. Specifically, we select the top 100 starred repositories with permissive open-source licenses (MIT, Apache 2.0, BSD 2-Clause, and BSD 3-Clause). From these repositories, we collect all pull requests (PRs) created after 2021 whose titles or descriptions reference a CVE identifier, yielding a coarse-grained pool of potential dependency-level vulnerabilities.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2.SSS0.Px2" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Rule-based Filtering

<div id="S3.SS2.SSS0.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para">

Because the initial candidate pool contains irrelevant cases, such as release notes and internal project vulnerabilities, we apply heuristic filters to remove low-quality candidates. Specifically, we first exclude cases in which all modified files are under test or example directories. Second, we exclude PRs that show more than five distinct CVEs. Finally, the most decisive rule requires the PR to modify a dependency manifest or lockfile (e.g., <span id="S3.SS2.SSS0.Px2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">go.mod</span>, <span id="S3.SS2.SSS0.Px2.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">requirements.txt</span>, <span id="S3.SS2.SSS0.Px2.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">setup.py</span>, <span id="S3.SS2.SSS0.Px2.p1.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">pom.xml</span>), which provides direct evidence that the host project actually consumes and reacts to the referenced vulnerable dependency. The PRs surviving this stage form the candidate pool for manual annotation.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2.SSS0.Px3" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Manual Annotation

<div id="S3.SS2.SSS0.Px3.p1" class="ltx_para">

The filtered candidates still require manual labeling, as no reliable automatic signal can serve as ground truth. Existing automated approaches are either inaccurate or require substantial manual effort, as discussed in <a href="#S2.SS1" class="ltx_ref" title="2.1 Software Supply Chain Security ‣ 2 Related Work ‣ VEX-Bench: Benchmarking LLM Agents for Assessing Exploitability of Software Supply Chain Vulnerabilities"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Section</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2.1</span></a>. Moreover, PR statuses on GitHub (e.g., merged or closed) cannot be directly interpreted as exploitability labels, since such decisions are often influenced by compatibility concerns or maintenance trade-offs. We therefore opt for manual annotation throughout.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2.SSS0.Px3.p2" class="ltx_para">

Five annotators with prior experience in software engineering and security manually analyze and label the candidate PRs, with each case taking roughly 30 minutes to annotate. To ensure inter-annotator consistency, we adopt a calibration-then-scaling protocol. In the calibration phase, all five annotators independently label the same 15 cases drawn across the three languages, achieving an initial Fleiss’ Kappa of 0.667. They then meet to reconcile disagreements and align on labeling criteria. In the scaling phase, annotators label distinct sets of cases under the calibrated guidelines, and each annotation is reviewed by at least one additional annotator. During review, 88.3% of the initial labels are confirmed without revision, while the remaining disagreements are resolved through discussion until consensus is reached. This process yields a final benchmark of 75 cases spanning Go, Python, and Java.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS3" class="section ltx_subsection">

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_table">Table 1: </span>This table reports the composition and codebase size of <span id="S3.T1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">VEX-Bench</span>, covering 75 cases across 67 CVEs and 35 real-world projects in Go, Python, and Java, where LOC denotes lines of code and Files denotes the number of source files in the target codebase.</figcaption>
</figure>

<figure id="S3.F2" class="ltx_figure">

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 2: </span>Label distribution of <span id="S3.F2.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">VEX-Bench</span> across 75 cases, where the inner ring shows the binary vulnerability status (affected vs. not affected) and the outer ring breaks down each status into its fine-grained justification category.</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S3.SS3.p1" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S3.SS3.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">VEX-Bench</span> contains 75 cases covering 67 CVEs drawn from 35 real-world repositories across Go, Python, and Java, as shown in <a href="#S3.T1" class="ltx_ref" title="In 3.3 Benchmark Statistics ‣ 3 VEX-Bench ‣ VEX-Bench: Benchmarking LLM Agents for Assessing Exploitability of Software Supply Chain Vulnerabilities"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Table</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>. The target codebases are full projects, not curated snippets. The median case spans roughly 273K lines of code across more than 2,600 files, and the Java subset is the largest, with a median of 546K LOC. An agent therefore cannot solve the task by scanning the entire project; it must locate the few dependency call sites relevant to the vulnerability within a large codebase. The cases also vary widely in scale, ranging from 9,902 LOC to about 4.0M, which prevents strong performance from being explained by overfitting to a single repository size.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS3.p2" class="ltx_para">

Most scanner-reported dependency vulnerabilities in <span id="S3.SS3.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">VEX-Bench</span> are not exploitable in the downstream project. <a href="#S3.F2" class="ltx_ref" title="In 3.3 Benchmark Statistics ‣ 3 VEX-Bench ‣ VEX-Bench: Benchmarking LLM Agents for Assessing Exploitability of Software Supply Chain Vulnerabilities"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Figure</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a> shows that only 29.3% of cases are genuinely <span id="S3.SS3.p2.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">affected</span>, while 70.7% are not. Within the <span id="S3.SS3.p2.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">not_affected</span> group, <span id="S3.SS3.p2.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">code_not_reachable</span> alone accounts for 38 cases, or 50.7% of the entire benchmark and 71.7% of all <span id="S3.SS3.p2.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">not_affected</span> cases. The remaining <span id="S3.SS3.p2.1.6" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">not_affected</span> cases are split across configuration, missing-code, and environment reasons. This long-tailed structure motivates fine-grained labels: a single <span id="S3.SS3.p2.1.7" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">not_affected</span> status would conflate reachability analysis with configuration, environment, and dependency reasoning, all of which require distinct agent capabilities.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS4" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">3.4 </span>Defense Against Benchmark Hacking

<div id="S3.SS4.p1" class="ltx_para">

As LLM agents become more capable and are granted broader execution privileges, recent work has shown that they can exploit weaknesses in benchmark environments, such as accessing hidden labels or tampering with evaluation logic to inflate reported scores (<a href="#bib.bib35" class="ltx_ref">Thaman, 2026</a>; <a href="#bib.bib36" class="ltx_ref">Atinafu and Cohen, 2026</a>). To mitigate these risks, we execute all experiments inside isolated Docker environments. Each container is separated from the benchmark labels and evaluation artifacts, ensuring that agents can only solve tasks through reasoning rather than by accessing ground-truth answers. In addition, our evaluation does not rely on executable test cases, eliminating the possibility of test-harness manipulation. To further prevent leakage through version history, we remove Git history and provide agents only with a standalone snapshot of the target commit. The result is a sealed environment with no path to the answer, and it is identical across runs, preserving reproducibility.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS4.p2" class="ltx_para">

Another potential source of leakage is web access. Since agents can search the web, they could in principle attempt to retrieve task-specific information online. However, the benchmark ground truth is not publicly available: all vulnerability-status labels and justifications are manually annotated by our security experts rather than derived from existing online sources. An agent may therefore gather background information about a vulnerability, but it cannot directly recover the benchmark answers. We further examine potential model-side leakage from the source PRs and find no meaningful evidence that they expose the benchmark answers or that the evaluated models memorized their contents. The full analysis is provided in Appendix <a href="#A3" class="ltx_ref" title="Appendix C Model-Side Leakage Analysis ‣ VEX-Bench: Benchmarking LLM Agents for Assessing Exploitability of Software Supply Chain Vulnerabilities"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">C</span></a>.

</div>

</div>

</div>

Best and second-best mean values are <span id="S4.T2.6" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">bolded</span> and <span id="S4.T2.7" class="ltx_text ltx_underline">underlined</span>, respectively; lower is better for Tokens/Cost.</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S4.SS1" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">4.1 </span>Experimental Setup

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Models and Harness

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">

Modern agents usually have two parts: a *model* that performs reasoning and a *harness* that manages execution, memory, tool use, and interaction with the environment. We therefore evaluate agents on <span id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">VEX-Bench</span> from both perspectives.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p2" class="ltx_para">

We select nine models, covering both closed-source and open-weight models. The closed-source group includes Claude Opus 4.6 (<a href="#bib.bib16" class="ltx_ref">Anthropic, 2026a</a>), Claude Sonnet 4.6 (<a href="#bib.bib37" class="ltx_ref">Anthropic, 2026b</a>), GPT-5.5 (<a href="#bib.bib17" class="ltx_ref">OpenAI, 2026b</a>), and GPT-5.4 mini (<a href="#bib.bib18" class="ltx_ref">OpenAI, 2026a</a>). The open-weight group includes five recent models reported to be competitive on agentic and coding benchmarks: Kimi K2.6 (<a href="#bib.bib19" class="ltx_ref">Moonshot AI, 2026</a>), DeepSeek-V4-Pro and DeepSeek-V4-Flash (<a href="#bib.bib20" class="ltx_ref">DeepSeek-AI, 2026</a>), MiniMax-M2.7 (<a href="#bib.bib21" class="ltx_ref">MiniMax, 2026</a>), and GLM-5.1 (<a href="#bib.bib22" class="ltx_ref">GLM-5-Team et al., 2026</a>).

</div>

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p3" class="ltx_para">

For harnesses, we evaluate both vendor-specific and open-source frameworks. Vendor-specific harnesses are paired with their corresponding models: Claude Code (<a href="#bib.bib23" class="ltx_ref">Anthropic, 2025</a>) with Claude Opus 4.6 and Claude Sonnet 4.6, and Codex (<a href="#bib.bib24" class="ltx_ref">OpenAI, 2025</a>) with GPT-5.5 and GPT-5.4 mini. We evaluate the open-weight models with OpenCode (<a href="#bib.bib25" class="ltx_ref">Anomaly Innovations (SST), 2025</a>), a widely used open-source agent harness. Details of these models and frameworks are in Appendix <a href="#A4" class="ltx_ref" title="Appendix D Experimental Details ‣ VEX-Bench: Benchmarking LLM Agents for Assessing Exploitability of Software Supply Chain Vulnerabilities"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">D</span></a>. Unless otherwise stated, model names below refer to the model under its corresponding default harness.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px2" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Metrics

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para">

We use classification metrics to assess predictions of vulnerability status and justification. The vulnerability status is a binary label indicating whether the codebase is affected by the vulnerability. We report standard binary classification metrics, including accuracy, precision, recall, and F1 score. The justification label is a multi-class label over the affected class and the four not-affected reasons, evaluating the agent’s fine-grained reasoning beyond the binary status prediction. We report accuracy, macro-F1, and weighted-F1 for this task.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px3" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Other Settings

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px3.p1" class="ltx_para">

To ensure reproducibility and prevent benchmark leakage, all experiments are conducted inside isolated Docker environments. We build separate Linux-based Docker images for different programming languages, each equipped with the corresponding build toolchains. The environment details are in Appendix <a href="#A4" class="ltx_ref" title="Appendix D Experimental Details ‣ VEX-Bench: Benchmarking LLM Agents for Assessing Exploitability of Software Supply Chain Vulnerabilities"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">D</span></a>.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px3.p2" class="ltx_para">

The task prompt contains a broader set of categories than the five annotated benchmark labels. Predictions outside the annotated label space are treated as <span id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px3.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">FAILED</span>. The complete prompt and label-mapping rules are provided in Appendix <a href="#A2.SS1" class="ltx_ref" title="B.1 Task Instruction Prompt ‣ Appendix B Prompts ‣ VEX-Bench: Benchmarking LLM Agents for Assessing Exploitability of Software Supply Chain Vulnerabilities"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">B.1</span></a>.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px3.p3" class="ltx_para">

We evaluate each model–harness configuration three times and report the mean and standard deviation across runs. To prevent excessively long or non-terminating executions, we impose a maximum runtime limit of 10 minutes per task. Executions that exceed this limit are terminated and recorded as timeouts. Overall, 98.2% of executions finish within this limit; timeouts are counted as failures and concentrate in MiniMax-M2.7 (12.0%) and Kimi-K2.6 (3.1%). Detailed runtime statistics are provided in Appendix <a href="#A4.SS4" class="ltx_ref" title="D.4 Runtime and Timeout Analysis ‣ Appendix D Experimental Details ‣ VEX-Bench: Benchmarking LLM Agents for Assessing Exploitability of Software Supply Chain Vulnerabilities"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">D.4</span></a>.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS2" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">4.2 </span>Main Results

<div id="S4.SS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

<a href="#S4.T2" class="ltx_ref" title="In 4 Evaluation ‣ VEX-Bench: Benchmarking LLM Agents for Assessing Exploitability of Software Supply Chain Vulnerabilities"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Table</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a> reports the performance of nine model and harness configurations. While current agents perform well on the coarse vulnerability-status task, they remain far from saturated on fine-grained justification. GPT-5.5 obtains the best vulnerability-status accuracy at 89.3%, followed by Claude Opus 4.6 at 88.4%. Claude Opus 4.6 achieves the best status recall and F1 score, reaching 87.9% recall and 81.6% F1, while GPT-5.5 obtains the best status precision at 88.7%. GPT-5.5 also leads all three justification metrics, reaching 78.2% accuracy, 73.5% macro-F1, and 81.5% weighted-F1. At the lower end, MiniMax-M2.7 has the lowest status accuracy at 71.1% and the lowest justification macro-F1 at 44.8%. DeepSeek-V4-Flash records the lowest status precision, recall, and F1 score, as well as the lowest justification accuracy and weighted-F1, reaching 58.7% justification accuracy and 62.8% weighted-F1.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS2.p2" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S4.SS2.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Performance consistently drops from vulnerability-status prediction to justification prediction.</span> For all nine configurations, justification accuracy is lower than status accuracy. The largest drop occurs for GPT-5.4 mini, from 82.2% status accuracy to 66.7% justification accuracy, a gap of 15.5 percentage points. GPT-5.5 and Claude Opus 4.6 also show clear drops of 11.1 and 12.0 points, respectively. Thus, reporting only the binary affected/not-affected status can overstate performance relative to the finer-grained reasoning needed for software supply-chain triage.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS3" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">4.3 </span>Comparison with Traditional SCA Tools

<figure id="S4.T3" class="ltx_table">
<table id="S4.T3.4" class="ltx_tabular ltx_centering ltx_guessed_headers ltx_align_middle">
<tbody class="ltx_tbody">
<tr id="S4.T3.4.1" class="odd ltx_tr">
<td id="S4.T3.4.1.1" class="ltx_td ltx_align_left ltx_th ltx_th_row ltx_border_tt"><span id="S4.T3.4.1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Method</span></td>
<td id="S4.T3.4.1.2" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center ltx_border_tt"><span id="S4.T3.4.1.2.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Cov.</span></td>

<td id="S4.T3.4.1.4" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center ltx_border_tt"><span id="S4.T3.4.1.4.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Prec.</span></td>
<td id="S4.T3.4.1.5" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center ltx_border_tt"><span id="S4.T3.4.1.5.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Rec.</span></td>
<td id="S4.T3.4.1.6" class="ltx_td ltx_nopad_r ltx_align_center ltx_border_tt"><span id="S4.T3.4.1.6.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">F1</span></td>
</tr>

</tr>

</tr>
<tr id="S4.T3.4.6" class="even ltx_tr">

<td id="S4.T3.4.6.2" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center">100.0</td>

<td id="S4.T3.4.6.6" class="ltx_td ltx_nopad_r ltx_align_center">38.9</td>
</tr>
<tr id="S4.T3.4.7" class="odd ltx_tr">
<td id="S4.T3.4.7.1" class="ltx_td ltx_align_left ltx_th ltx_th_row">OSV (reach.)</td>
<td id="S4.T3.4.7.2" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center">100.0</td>
<td id="S4.T3.4.7.3" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center">46.7</td>
<td id="S4.T3.4.7.4" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center">28.6</td>
<td id="S4.T3.4.7.5" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center">85.7</td>
<td id="S4.T3.4.7.6" class="ltx_td ltx_nopad_r ltx_align_center">42.9</td>
</tr>

<td id="S4.T3.4.8.1" class="ltx_td ltx_align_left ltx_th ltx_th_row ltx_border_bb">govulncheck</td>
<td id="S4.T3.4.8.2" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center ltx_border_bb">63.3</td>

<td id="S4.T3.4.8.4" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center ltx_border_bb">22.2</td>

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_table">Table 3: </span>Performance of traditional SCA baselines on <span id="S4.T3.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">VEX-Bench</span>. Cov., Acc., Prec., and Rec. denote coverage, accuracy, precision, and recall, respectively. Coverage is the percentage of repositories successfully analyzed. OSV (detect.) uses OSV-Scanner for dependency detection, whereas OSV (reach.) enables its vulnerable-function reachability analysis. The full benchmark contains all 75 cases, and the Go subset contains 30 cases. All values are percentages.</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S4.SS3.p1" class="ltx_para">

To assess the performance of traditional software composition analysis (SCA) tools, we select Trivy <a href="#bib.bib39" class="ltx_ref">Aqua Security ()</a> and OSV-Scanner <a href="#bib.bib40" class="ltx_ref">Google ()</a>, two widely used tools, as baselines on the full dataset. We further evaluate OSV-Scanner’s reachability mode and govulncheck <a href="#bib.bib41" class="ltx_ref">The Go Authors ()</a> on the 30 Go cases, as reachability analysis requires mappings from CVEs to vulnerable functions, and reliable function-level records are available only for Go. We predict <span id="S4.SS3.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">affected</span> if a tool reports the target CVE and <span id="S4.SS3.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">not affected</span> otherwise.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS3.p2" class="ltx_para">

<a href="#S4.T3" class="ltx_ref" title="In 4.3 Comparison with Traditional SCA Tools ‣ 4 Evaluation ‣ VEX-Bench: Benchmarking LLM Agents for Assessing Exploitability of Software Supply Chain Vulnerabilities"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Table</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3</span></a> reports the performance of traditional SCA baselines on <span id="S4.SS3.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">VEX-Bench</span>. Traditional SCA tools can flag a broad set of potential vulnerabilities, but their low precision shows that they cannot reliably distinguish affected projects from false alerts. Reachability analysis does not close this gap. Govulncheck requires the target project to build successfully and therefore completes only 19 of the 30 cases. Moreover, reachability tools determine whether a vulnerable function appears in the call graph, but this does not establish whether the corresponding path is feasible or whether its branch conditions can actually trigger the vulnerable behavior. This limitation, together with the difficulty of constructing call graphs that are both sound and precise, explains why 70% of their false positives are still labeled <span id="S4.SS3.p2.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">code_not_reachable</span> and prevents reachability analysis from reliably assessing exploitability.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS4" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">4.4 </span>Analysis Results

<div id="S4.SS4.SSS1" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">4.4.1 </span>Cost-Performance Analysis

<figure id="S4.F3" class="ltx_figure">

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 3: </span>Performance versus per-case inference cost on <span id="S4.F3.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">VEX-Bench</span>. The x-axis uses a log scale, and the line connects Pareto-efficient configurations.</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S4.SS4.SSS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S4.SS4.SSS1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Performance generally improves with inference cost, but the gains show diminishing returns.</span> <a href="#S4.F3" class="ltx_ref" title="In 4.4.1 Cost-Performance Analysis ‣ 4.4 Analysis Results ‣ 4 Evaluation ‣ VEX-Bench: Benchmarking LLM Agents for Assessing Exploitability of Software Supply Chain Vulnerabilities"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Figure</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3</span></a> plots per-case cost against a balanced performance score $P = {\frac{1}{2}\hspace{0pt}{({{{({A_{s} + F_{s}})}/2} + {{({A_{j} + M_{j} + W_{j}})}/3}})}}$, where $A_{s}$ and $F_{s}$ denote status accuracy and status F1, and $A_{j}$, $M_{j}$, and $W_{j}$ denote justification accuracy, macro-F1, and weighted-F1. GPT-5.5 achieves the highest performance at 81.2%, but is also the most expensive configuration at \$1.082 per case. Claude Opus 4.6 follows at 78.5% and costs \$0.595 per case. In contrast, DeepSeek-V4-Pro reaches 74.4% at only \$0.075 per case, and GLM-5.1 reaches 74.9% at \$0.095 per case. These results suggest that inference cost is an incomplete proxy for exploitability-analysis quality: GPT-5.5 and Claude Opus 4.6 are strongest in absolute performance, while DeepSeek-V4-Pro and GLM-5.1 provide better cost-performance trade-offs.

</div>

<figure id="S4.F4" class="ltx_figure">

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 4: </span>Error breakdown by ground-truth output category for four representative configurations. Each bar shows the average number of incorrect predictions per run, stacked by the category predicted by the agent. Categories with fewer than three benchmark cases are omitted.</figcaption>
</figure>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS4.SSS2" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">4.4.2 </span>Language Analysis

<figure id="S4.F5" class="ltx_figure">

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 5: </span>Per-language F1 for four representative configurations. (a) Status F1. (b) Justification macro-F1.</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S4.SS4.SSS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S4.SS4.SSS2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Performance varies across programming languages.</span> <a href="#S4.F5" class="ltx_ref" title="In 4.4.2 Language Analysis ‣ 4.4 Analysis Results ‣ 4 Evaluation ‣ VEX-Bench: Benchmarking LLM Agents for Assessing Exploitability of Software Supply Chain Vulnerabilities"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Figure</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5</span></a> breaks down performance by language for four representative configurations. Averaged over these configurations, status F1 ranges from 71.3% on Python to 81.3% on Go, a 10.0-point spread. In contrast, justification macro-F1 ranges from 56.5% on Python to 75.5% on Go, a 19.0-point spread. Go achieves the highest average justification macro-F1, while Python is the weakest language on both justification macro-F1 and average status F1. This suggests that language ecosystems affect agents’ ability to identify the correct exploitability reason. One possible explanation is that Go has a comparatively mature and manageable package ecosystem, whereas Java projects often involve more complex multi-module dependency structures and Python projects frequently rely on loosely specified dependencies.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS4.SSS3" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">4.4.3 </span>Error Analysis

<div id="S4.SS4.SSS3.p1" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S4.SS4.SSS3.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Models share where they fail but differ in how they fail.</span> <a href="#S4.F4" class="ltx_ref" title="In 4.4.1 Cost-Performance Analysis ‣ 4.4 Analysis Results ‣ 4 Evaluation ‣ VEX-Bench: Benchmarking LLM Agents for Assessing Exploitability of Software Supply Chain Vulnerabilities"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Figure</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4</span></a> shows that errors across representative configurations concentrate in <span id="S4.SS4.SSS3.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">code_not_reachable</span> (CNR) and <span id="S4.SS4.SSS3.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">vulnerable</span> cases, yet the misclassification patterns diverge by model. <span id="S4.SS4.SSS3.p1.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">FAILED</span> denotes runs that do not produce a valid, evaluable output because of a timeout, a parsing failure, or a prediction outside the five annotated classes. GPT-5.5 frequently predicts <span id="S4.SS4.SSS3.p1.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">requires_configuration</span> for CNR cases. We manually reviewed the five cases with this error pattern and found that four admit both explanations: the default configuration does not activate a path to the vulnerable functionality, whereas a non-default configuration can activate the corresponding component. Under our precedence rule, these cases are labeled <span id="S4.SS4.SSS3.p1.1.6" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">code_not_reachable</span>; nevertheless, <span id="S4.SS4.SSS3.p1.1.7" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">requires_configuration</span> captures an additional condition supported by the evidence. These predictions therefore violate the required precedence but may still be factually valid. Claude Opus 4.6, in contrast, often over-calls CNR cases as <span id="S4.SS4.SSS3.p1.1.8" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">vulnerable</span>, indicating a more aggressive tendency to treat dependency evidence as exploitable risk. DeepSeek-V4-Pro exhibits a more balanced but less decisive profile, with comparable error counts on <span id="S4.SS4.SSS3.p1.1.9" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">vulnerable</span> and CNR cases, suggesting an unstable boundary between truly affected cases and non-exploitable dependency paths. Across models, errors cluster near the operational boundary between filtering false dependency alerts and identifying truly affected projects.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS4.SSS4" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">4.4.4 </span>Repository Size Analysis

<figure id="S4.F6" class="ltx_figure">

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 6: </span>Status F1 and justification macro-F1 as a function of target repository size for four representative configurations. Each point is computed over a 25-task sliding window sorted by language-specific repository LOC. Shaded regions show run-to-run standard deviation when available.</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S4.SS4.SSS4.p1" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S4.SS4.SSS4.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Repository size is not the primary bottleneck for strong agents, but it makes fine-grained reasoning less stable.</span> <a href="#S4.F6" class="ltx_ref" title="In 4.4.4 Repository Size Analysis ‣ 4.4 Analysis Results ‣ 4 Evaluation ‣ VEX-Bench: Benchmarking LLM Agents for Assessing Exploitability of Software Supply Chain Vulnerabilities"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Figure</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">6</span></a> examines 25-task sliding windows ranging from about 44K to 1.9M lines of code. GPT-5.5 and Claude Opus 4.6 maintain high status F1 in the largest windows, reaching 85.0% and 84.5%, respectively. DeepSeek-V4-Pro is more sensitive to repository size, dropping from 83.2% status F1 in the smallest window to 71.0% in the largest window. Across models, the justification macro-F1 curves are lower and more variable than the status-F1 curves, especially in large repositories. This indicates that scaling to larger codebases mainly stresses the agent’s ability to localize and explain the relevant dependency path, rather than only its ability to make a binary affected/not-affected decision.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS4.SSS5" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">4.4.5 </span>Harness Ablation Study

<figure id="S4.F7" class="ltx_figure">

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 7: </span>Ablation on the harness for GPT-5.4 mini and GPT-5.5 on <span id="S4.F7.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">VEX-Bench</span>. Each row shows one model evaluated under the Codex CLI and OpenCode harnesses, reporting vulnerability-status accuracy/F1, justification accuracy/F1, and average per-case token usage (in thousands).</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S4.SS4.SSS5.p1" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S4.SS4.SSS5.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Harness frameworks yield modest performance differences but substantially different token costs.</span> <a href="#S4.F7" class="ltx_ref" title="In 4.4.5 Harness Ablation Study ‣ 4.4 Analysis Results ‣ 4 Evaluation ‣ VEX-Bench: Benchmarking LLM Agents for Assessing Exploitability of Software Supply Chain Vulnerabilities"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Figure</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">7</span></a> compares GPT-5.4 mini and GPT-5.5 under Codex CLI and OpenCode. For GPT-5.5, Codex CLI consistently outperforms OpenCode across all metrics, but at the cost of more than doubling per-case token usage. For GPT-5.4 mini, the two harnesses are comparable in performance, yet Codex CLI consumes roughly 4$\times$ more tokens. Overall, harness choice affects inference cost far more than classification performance.

</div>

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S5" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">5 </span>Conclusion

<div id="S5.p1" class="ltx_para">

We introduced <span id="S5.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">VEX-Bench</span>, the first benchmark designed to evaluate whether LLM agents can assess the exploitability of software supply chain vulnerabilities. The benchmark comprises 75 real-world cases drawn from Go, Python, and Java projects, with ground-truth labels manually annotated by security experts. Our evaluation of nine model–harness configurations shows that these systems perform better on binary vulnerability-status classification than on the more fine-grained task of determining justification status. Overall, the results suggest that LLM agents are effective at identifying whether a project is affected by a vulnerability but still lack the depth of analysis required to provide accurate justifications.

</div>

</div>

<div id="Sx1" class="section ltx_section">

## Limitations

<div id="Sx1.p1" class="ltx_para">

<span id="Sx1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">VEX-Bench</span> has three main limitations. First, its scale and coverage are constrained by the high cost of expert annotation. The benchmark contains 75 cases across three language ecosystems, and some fine-grained justification categories contain only a small number of examples. Moreover, the current dataset covers only four types of not-affected justifications. This dataset also does not include other widely used ecosystems, such as JavaScript and npm. Consequently, the reported results may not fully capture the diversity of exploitability assessment challenges encountered in practice, particularly at the fine-grained justification level.

</div>

<div id="Sx1.p2" class="ltx_para">

Second, a single case may admit multiple valid explanations. We apply a predefined precedence rule to assign one gold label and make evaluation unambiguous. Nevertheless, this single-label formulation may omit valid secondary reasons for why a project is not affected.

</div>

<div id="Sx1.p3" class="ltx_para">

Third, <span id="Sx1.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">VEX-Bench</span> assesses repository snapshots using the source code and configuration information available in the repository. Such static evidence cannot fully capture dynamic user inputs or deployment-specific environment settings. The resulting judgments therefore approximate exploitability under the repository’s documented or default conditions and may not reflect every real-world deployment.

</div>

</div>

<div id="Sx2" class="section ltx_section">

## Ethical Considerations

<div id="Sx2.p1" class="ltx_para">

Research on vulnerability exploitability has an inherent dual-use risk. In principle, the ability to determine whether a vulnerable dependency is reachable in a downstream project could help malicious actors prioritize known vulnerabilities for further investigation. However, <span id="Sx2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">VEX-Bench</span> uses only publicly disclosed CVEs and does not involve zero-day discovery. Its tasks ask agents to classify exploitability and justify their decisions with code and advisory evidence; they do not require exploit generation, payload construction, or attacks against running services. In addition, all experiments analyze source-code snapshots inside isolated Docker environments without access to deployed systems, reducing their direct operational value for attacks and the risk of affecting real-world infrastructure. <span id="Sx2.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">VEX-Bench</span> is intended for defensive vulnerability triage, helping defenders prioritize genuine risks, reduce false-positive alerts, and evaluate the capabilities of LLM agents.

</div>

</div>

<div id="Sx3" class="section ltx_section">

## Acknowledgments

<div id="Sx3.p1" class="ltx_para">

We sincerely thank the anonymous reviewers for their constructive feedback. We also thank Theodor Mihalache, Shimon Tanny, Tamar Weisskopf, Michelle DiPalma, and Jude Niroshan at Red Hat for their contributions to this work. This work was supported in part by NSF Proto-OKN Award 2333736.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A1" class="section ltx_appendix">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_appendix">Appendix A </span>Justification Category Definitions

<div id="A1.p1" class="ltx_para">

<span id="A1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">VEX-Bench</span> reports four justification categories that explain why a downstream project is *not* affected by a known vulnerability in one of its dependencies. <a href="#A1.T4" class="ltx_ref" title="In Appendix A Justification Category Definitions ‣ VEX-Bench: Benchmarking LLM Agents for Assessing Exploitability of Software Supply Chain Vulnerabilities"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Table</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4</span></a> summarizes the four categories at a glance; the paragraphs that follow describe, for each category, the underlying condition, the evidence required to assign it, and a representative case from <span id="A1.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">VEX-Bench</span>.

</div>

<figure id="A1.T4" class="ltx_table">
<table id="A1.T4.6" class="ltx_tabular ltx_centering ltx_guessed_headers ltx_align_middle">
<thead class="ltx_thead">
<tr id="A1.T4.6.1" class="header ltx_tr">
<th id="A1.T4.6.1.1" class="ltx_td ltx_align_left ltx_align_top ltx_th ltx_th_column ltx_border_tt"><span id="A1.T4.6.1.1.1" class="ltx_inline-block ltx_align_top" style="width:75.9pt;"> <span id="A1.T4.6.1.1.1.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.T4.6.1.1.1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Category</span></span> </span></th>
<th id="A1.T4.6.1.2" class="ltx_td ltx_align_left ltx_align_top ltx_th ltx_th_column ltx_border_tt"><span id="A1.T4.6.1.2.1" class="ltx_inline-block ltx_align_top" style="width:255.3pt;"> <span id="A1.T4.6.1.2.1.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.T4.6.1.2.1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Definition</span></span> </span></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody class="ltx_tbody">
<tr id="A1.T4.6.2" class="odd ltx_tr">
<td id="A1.T4.6.2.1" class="ltx_td ltx_align_left ltx_align_top ltx_border_t"><span id="A1.T4.6.2.1.1" class="ltx_inline-block ltx_align_top" style="width:75.9pt;"> <span id="A1.T4.6.2.1.1.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.T4.6.2.1.1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">code_not_present</span></span> </span></td>
<td id="A1.T4.6.2.2" class="ltx_td ltx_align_left ltx_align_top ltx_border_t"><span id="A1.T4.6.2.2.1" class="ltx_inline-block ltx_align_top" style="width:255.3pt;"> <span id="A1.T4.6.2.2.1.1" class="ltx_p">The vulnerable package or module is absent from the project’s resolved dependency graph: not declared in any manifest, not pinned in any lockfile, not vendored, and not pulled in transitively.</span> </span></td>
</tr>
<tr id="A1.T4.6.3" class="even ltx_tr">
<td id="A1.T4.6.3.1" class="ltx_td ltx_align_left ltx_align_top"><span id="A1.T4.6.3.1.1" class="ltx_inline-block ltx_align_top" style="width:75.9pt;"> <span id="A1.T4.6.3.1.1.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.T4.6.3.1.1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">code_not_reachable</span></span> </span></td>
<td id="A1.T4.6.3.2" class="ltx_td ltx_align_left ltx_align_top"><span id="A1.T4.6.3.2.1" class="ltx_inline-block ltx_align_top" style="width:255.3pt;"> <span id="A1.T4.6.3.2.1.1" class="ltx_p">The vulnerable function exists somewhere in the dependency tree but is never invoked along any reachable call path from first-party code.</span> </span></td>
</tr>
<tr id="A1.T4.6.4" class="odd ltx_tr">
<td id="A1.T4.6.4.1" class="ltx_td ltx_align_left ltx_align_top"><span id="A1.T4.6.4.1.1" class="ltx_inline-block ltx_align_top" style="width:75.9pt;"> <span id="A1.T4.6.4.1.1.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.T4.6.4.1.1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">requires_configuration</span></span> </span></td>
<td id="A1.T4.6.4.2" class="ltx_td ltx_align_left ltx_align_top"><span id="A1.T4.6.4.2.1" class="ltx_inline-block ltx_align_top" style="width:255.3pt;"> <span id="A1.T4.6.4.2.1.1" class="ltx_p">The vulnerable code path is reachable, but exploitation requires a non-default configuration option, feature flag, or runtime setting that the project does not enable.</span> </span></td>
</tr>
<tr id="A1.T4.6.5" class="even ltx_tr">
<td id="A1.T4.6.5.1" class="ltx_td ltx_align_left ltx_align_top ltx_border_bb"><span id="A1.T4.6.5.1.1" class="ltx_inline-block ltx_align_top" style="width:75.9pt;"> <span id="A1.T4.6.5.1.1.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.T4.6.5.1.1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">requires_environment</span></span> </span></td>
<td id="A1.T4.6.5.2" class="ltx_td ltx_align_left ltx_align_top ltx_border_bb"><span id="A1.T4.6.5.2.1" class="ltx_inline-block ltx_align_top" style="width:255.3pt;"> <span id="A1.T4.6.5.2.1.1" class="ltx_p">The vulnerable code is reachable, but its trigger depends on a runtime environment (operating system, kernel feature, hardware capability, or external service) that the project does not establish.</span> </span></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_table">Table 4: </span>The four <span id="A1.T4.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">not_affected</span> justification categories used in <span id="A1.T4.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">VEX-Bench</span>, adapted from the VEX status-justification vocabulary (<a href="#bib.bib27" class="ltx_ref">CISA, 2022</a>).</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="A1.p2" class="ltx_para">

Although <span id="A1.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">VEX-Bench</span> reports four justification categories in the main benchmark, we considered a broader VEX-aligned category space when designing the task prompt. The additional categories listed in <a href="#A1.T5" class="ltx_ref" title="In Appendix A Justification Category Definitions ‣ VEX-Bench: Benchmarking LLM Agents for Assessing Exploitability of Software Supply Chain Vulnerabilities"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Table</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5</span></a> represent theoretically possible not-affected reasons and boundary cases drawn from the status-justification vocabulary maintained by CISA for VEX (<a href="#bib.bib27" class="ltx_ref">CISA, 2022</a>). They document the prompt design, but are not introduced as separate benchmark classes. All dataset statistics and justification metrics in the paper are reported over the four categories above.

</div>

<figure id="A1.T5" class="ltx_table">
<table id="A1.T5.2" class="ltx_tabular ltx_centering ltx_guessed_headers ltx_align_middle">
<thead class="ltx_thead">
<tr id="A1.T5.2.1" class="header ltx_tr">
<th id="A1.T5.2.1.1" class="ltx_td ltx_align_left ltx_align_top ltx_th ltx_th_column ltx_border_tt"><span id="A1.T5.2.1.1.1" class="ltx_inline-block ltx_align_top" style="width:89.7pt;"> <span id="A1.T5.2.1.1.1.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.T5.2.1.1.1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Category</span></span> </span></th>
<th id="A1.T5.2.1.2" class="ltx_td ltx_align_left ltx_align_top ltx_th ltx_th_column ltx_border_tt"><span id="A1.T5.2.1.2.1" class="ltx_inline-block ltx_align_top" style="width:241.5pt;"> <span id="A1.T5.2.1.2.1.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.T5.2.1.2.1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Definition</span></span> </span></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody class="ltx_tbody">
<tr id="A1.T5.2.2" class="odd ltx_tr">
<td id="A1.T5.2.2.1" class="ltx_td ltx_align_left ltx_align_top ltx_border_t"><span id="A1.T5.2.2.1.1" class="ltx_inline-block ltx_align_top" style="width:89.7pt;"> <span id="A1.T5.2.2.1.1.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.T5.2.2.1.1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">requires_dependency</span></span> </span></td>
<td id="A1.T5.2.2.2" class="ltx_td ltx_align_left ltx_align_top ltx_border_t"><span id="A1.T5.2.2.2.1" class="ltx_inline-block ltx_align_top" style="width:241.5pt;"> <span id="A1.T5.2.2.2.1.1" class="ltx_p">Exploitation requires an additional optional dependency (library, plugin, or module) that the project does not declare.</span> </span></td>
</tr>
<tr id="A1.T5.2.3" class="even ltx_tr">
<td id="A1.T5.2.3.1" class="ltx_td ltx_align_left ltx_align_top"><span id="A1.T5.2.3.1.1" class="ltx_inline-block ltx_align_top" style="width:89.7pt;"> <span id="A1.T5.2.3.1.1.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.T5.2.3.1.1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">compiler_protected</span></span> </span></td>
<td id="A1.T5.2.3.2" class="ltx_td ltx_align_left ltx_align_top"><span id="A1.T5.2.3.2.1" class="ltx_inline-block ltx_align_top" style="width:241.5pt;"> <span id="A1.T5.2.3.2.1.1" class="ltx_p">Compile-time hardening configured in the project’s build pipeline (stack canaries, PIE/ASLR-enforced builds, sanitizer instrumentation) prevents the exploit primitive.</span> </span></td>
</tr>
<tr id="A1.T5.2.4" class="odd ltx_tr">
<td id="A1.T5.2.4.1" class="ltx_td ltx_align_left ltx_align_top"><span id="A1.T5.2.4.1.1" class="ltx_inline-block ltx_align_top" style="width:89.7pt;"> <span id="A1.T5.2.4.1.1.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.T5.2.4.1.1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">runtime_protected</span></span> </span></td>
<td id="A1.T5.2.4.2" class="ltx_td ltx_align_left ltx_align_top"><span id="A1.T5.2.4.2.1" class="ltx_inline-block ltx_align_top" style="width:241.5pt;"> <span id="A1.T5.2.4.2.1.1" class="ltx_p">Runtime mechanisms instantiated by the project’s own code (sandboxing, in-process isolation, seccomp filters) prevent exploitation.</span> </span></td>
</tr>
<tr id="A1.T5.2.5" class="even ltx_tr">
<td id="A1.T5.2.5.1" class="ltx_td ltx_align_left ltx_align_top"><span id="A1.T5.2.5.1.1" class="ltx_inline-block ltx_align_top" style="width:89.7pt;"> <span id="A1.T5.2.5.1.1.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.T5.2.5.1.1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">perimeter_protected</span></span> </span></td>
<td id="A1.T5.2.5.2" class="ltx_td ltx_align_left ltx_align_top"><span id="A1.T5.2.5.2.1" class="ltx_inline-block ltx_align_top" style="width:241.5pt;"> <span id="A1.T5.2.5.2.1.1" class="ltx_p">Network, authentication, or perimeter controls shipped with the project (authentication middleware, allowlists, deployment-level network policies) block the attack surface.</span> </span></td>
</tr>
<tr id="A1.T5.2.6" class="odd ltx_tr">
<td id="A1.T5.2.6.1" class="ltx_td ltx_align_left ltx_align_top ltx_border_bb"><span id="A1.T5.2.6.1.1" class="ltx_inline-block ltx_align_top" style="width:89.7pt;"> <span id="A1.T5.2.6.1.1.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.T5.2.6.1.1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">mitigating_control_protected</span></span> </span></td>
<td id="A1.T5.2.6.2" class="ltx_td ltx_align_left ltx_align_top ltx_border_bb"><span id="A1.T5.2.6.2.1" class="ltx_inline-block ltx_align_top" style="width:241.5pt;"> <span id="A1.T5.2.6.2.1.1" class="ltx_p">Other in-repository safeguards not covered above (input validation, output encoding, custom guards) reduce risk to negligible.</span> </span></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_table">Table 5: </span>Broader VEX-aligned categories considered during prompt construction. These categories represent theoretically possible not-affected reasons and boundary cases, but are not separate benchmark classes in the reported evaluation.</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="A1.SS0.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### code_not_present

<div id="A1.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">

The vulnerable package or module is absent from the project’s resolved dependency graph: not declared in any manifest, not pinned in any lockfile, not vendored, and not pulled in transitively. This label is assigned only after inspecting every dependency manifest, the corresponding lockfiles, and any vendored or generated sources, since a vulnerability database entry for the broader package family does not by itself imply that the affected module is consumed by this project. *Example.* CVE-2024-7254 in <span id="A1.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">apache/dubbo</span> at commit <span id="A1.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">218aab1</span>: although the underlying library appears in upstream advisories, the affected module is not part of the project’s resolved dependencies, so no call path can reach the vulnerable code.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A1.SS0.SSS0.Px2" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### code_not_reachable

<div id="A1.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para">

The vulnerable function is present somewhere in the dependency tree but is never invoked along any reachable call path from first-party code. The vulnerable surface may live in a transitive dependency, in an unused submodule of a direct dependency, or behind an import path that the project does not exercise. Assigning this label requires identifying the vulnerable functions named in the advisory or its fix and verifying that no import, reflection edge, or call site in first-party source leads to them. *Example.* CVE-2025-55752 in <span id="A1.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">alibaba/nacos</span> at commit <span id="A1.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">20caf99</span>: the affected library is included in the dependency graph, but the specific APIs that the patch modifies are not referenced anywhere in the project’s source.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A1.SS0.SSS0.Px3" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### requires_configuration

<div id="A1.SS0.SSS0.Px3.p1" class="ltx_para">

The vulnerable code path exists and is reachable, but exploitation requires a non-default configuration option, feature flag, or runtime setting that the project does not enable. The controlling configuration may live in a YAML or properties file shipped with the project, an environment variable read at startup, or a hard-coded constant in first-party code. Assigning this label requires locating the controlling configuration source, confirming that its disabling value is in effect by default, and checking that no other code path overrides it at runtime. *Example.* CVE-2023-47248 in <span id="A1.SS0.SSS0.Px3.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">apache/airflow</span> at commit <span id="A1.SS0.SSS0.Px3.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">3dbad4f</span>: the vulnerable serialization behavior is gated by a configuration switch whose default value disables the affected functionality.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A1.SS0.SSS0.Px4" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### requires_environment

<div id="A1.SS0.SSS0.Px4.p1" class="ltx_para">

The vulnerable code is reachable but its trigger depends on a runtime environment that the project does not establish. The required environment may be an operating system, kernel feature, hardware capability, or external service that the project’s deployment manifests, Dockerfiles, or installation instructions do not provide. Assigning this label requires reading the advisory’s precondition description and confirming that the host project’s declared environment lies outside the affected range. *Example.* CVE-2024-47535 in <span id="A1.SS0.SSS0.Px4.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">prestodb/presto</span> at commit <span id="A1.SS0.SSS0.Px4.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">8136f7c</span>: the exploit’s required runtime environment is not part of the project’s supported deployment surface.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="A2" class="section ltx_appendix">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_appendix">Appendix B </span>Prompts

<div id="A2.SS1" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">B.1 </span>Task Instruction Prompt

<div id="A2.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

The prompt below reflects the broader VEX-aligned category space considered during prompt construction. It should be read as the instruction template used to make the agent’s decision process explicit; the benchmark label space reported in the paper remains the four categories defined in <a href="#A1.T4" class="ltx_ref" title="In Appendix A Justification Category Definitions ‣ VEX-Bench: Benchmarking LLM Agents for Assessing Exploitability of Software Supply Chain Vulnerabilities"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Table</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4</span></a>.

</div>

<div id="A2.SS1.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Mapping to Benchmark Labels.

<div id="A2.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">

Although the prompt allows 12 categories, <span id="A2.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">VEX-Bench</span> evaluates only five annotated classes: <span id="A2.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">vulnerable</span> and the four not-affected categories defined in Appendix <a href="#A1" class="ltx_ref" title="Appendix A Justification Category Definitions ‣ VEX-Bench: Benchmarking LLM Agents for Assessing Exploitability of Software Supply Chain Vulnerabilities"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">A</span></a>. A <span id="A2.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">vulnerable</span> prediction maps to the affected status, while the four annotated justification categories map to the not-affected status. Predictions in any of the remaining categories are marked as <span id="A2.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">FAILED</span>; they are neither remapped to an annotated class nor excluded from evaluation.

</div>

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="A3" class="section ltx_appendix">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_appendix">Appendix C </span>Model-Side Leakage Analysis

<div id="A3.p1" class="ltx_para">

Because <span id="A3.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">VEX-Bench</span> is constructed from public repositories, the source PRs may have appeared in model training data even though the benchmark labels are not public. We assess this potential source of leakage through a manual audit of the source PRs and a guided-completion probe for memorized PR content.

</div>

<div id="A3.SS0.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Source PR Audit

<div id="A3.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">

We manually review the titles, descriptions, and discussion threads of the source PRs associated with all 75 cases. Only three discuss whether the corresponding vulnerability is exploitable, and none contains the vulnerability status or justification assigned by our annotators. The public source PRs therefore do not directly reveal the benchmark answers.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A3.SS0.SSS0.Px2" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Guided-Completion Probe

<div id="A3.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para">

Following prior work (<a href="#bib.bib38" class="ltx_ref">Golchin and Surdeanu, 2024</a>), we further probe whether the evaluated models reproduce source PR content from memory. We select PRs that were created before the models’ knowledge cutoff dates, written by humans rather than bots, non-boilerplate, and sufficiently long to support a meaningful completion test. Nineteen distinct PRs satisfy these criteria. We test all of them with GPT-5.5 and Claude Opus 4.6, with web access and tools disabled, and classify each response as an exact match, a verified near-exact match, a nonmatching completion, or a refusal/empty response.

</div>

<figure id="A3.T6" class="ltx_table">
<table id="A3.T6.2" class="ltx_tabular ltx_centering ltx_guessed_headers ltx_align_middle">
<thead class="ltx_thead">

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_table">Table 6: </span>Results of the guided-completion probe on the 19 source PRs selected for model-side leakage analysis.</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="A3.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p2" class="ltx_para">

Across the 38 completions, we observe no verified near-exact matches and only one exact ten-token continuation. This match occurs because the PR body repeats its title, which is already provided in the prompt, rather than because the model reproduces unseen PR content. Together with the source PR audit, these results provide no meaningful evidence that the evaluated models memorized the source PR contents or could recover the benchmark answers from them.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<td id="A4.T7.2.4.4" class="ltx_td ltx_align_left ltx_border_bb">OpenJDK <span id="A4.T7.2.4.4.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">21.0.11</span>; Maven <span id="A4.T7.2.4.4.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">3.9.12</span></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_table">Table 7: </span>Language runtime environments used in our Docker containers.</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="A4.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

Table <a href="#A4.T7" class="ltx_ref" title="Table 7 ‣ D.1 Environment ‣ Appendix D Experimental Details ‣ VEX-Bench: Benchmarking LLM Agents for Assessing Exploitability of Software Supply Chain Vulnerabilities"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">7</span></a> summarizes the language-level runtime environments used in our Docker containers. We report only the base image, operating system, and primary runtime or build tool versions; common utilities such as <span id="A4.SS1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">git</span>, <span id="A4.SS1.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">curl</span>, and <span id="A4.SS1.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">ca-certificates</span> are inherited or installed as part of the container setup.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A4.SS2" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">D.2 </span>Model Backends

<div id="A4.SS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

All model API calls in our experiments were routed through Azure’s model-serving APIs. We used the Azure AI Model Inference REST API as the common provider interface for deployed foundation models (<a href="#bib.bib26" class="ltx_ref">Microsoft, 2026</a>). Table <a href="#A4.T8" class="ltx_ref" title="Table 8 ‣ D.2 Model Backends ‣ Appendix D Experimental Details ‣ VEX-Bench: Benchmarking LLM Agents for Assessing Exploitability of Software Supply Chain Vulnerabilities"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">8</span></a> lists the model names used in the paper, the corresponding provider-facing model card or release source, and the public release date of each model.

</div>

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_table">Table 9: </span>Agent harness versions and effort settings used in our experiments.</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="A4.SS3.p1" class="ltx_para">

Table <a href="#A4.T9" class="ltx_ref" title="Table 9 ‣ D.3 Harness Framework ‣ Appendix D Experimental Details ‣ VEX-Bench: Benchmarking LLM Agents for Assessing Exploitability of Software Supply Chain Vulnerabilities"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">9</span></a> reports the harness frameworks used in our experiments. For each agent, we list the corresponding official website or documentation page, the exact CLI version installed in the experimental containers, and the effort setting used when the harness exposes such a control. We used the strongest available effort setting in our setup: Codex was run with <span id="A4.SS3.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">xhigh</span>, and Claude Code was run with <span id="A4.SS3.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">high</span>. For the Claude 4.6 models, <span id="A4.SS3.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">high</span> is the maximum supported effort level. OpenCode was run without an effort override because no analogous effort setting was configured in our experiments. These versions and settings identify the agent-side execution environment; model identifiers and provider backends are reported separately from the tool versions.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A4.SS4" class="section ltx_subsection">
